## 5. Generalized application-world contract

The leading design is an exact application world with typed exports and typed imports.

The implementation may improve the shape.

It must satisfy the invariants in this section.

### 5.1 World identity

An application world is immutable exact application meaning.

Its identity is distinct from:

- release identity;
- application artifact digest;
- interface identity;
- instance identity;
- grant identity;
- adapter identity;
- deployment identity;
- executable identity;
- and cache identity.

If the complete application artifact digest remains exact application identity, state that narrowly.

Do not infer publisher, provenance, authorization, freshness, or grant from a digest.

### 5.2 Stateful export

A stateful world should name exact nominal types for:

- state;
- event;
- response;
- command;
- outcome;
- and decision.

The leading decision shape is a nominal sum:

```text
Decision =
    completed { state: State, response: Response }
  | suspended { state: State, response: Response, command: Command }
```

The exact syntax and metadata may differ.

The structural intent must remain typed.

Do not retain a magic integer command discriminator when a nominal sum already exists.

The resume function should consume a typed outcome compatible with the pending command.

At most one pending command permits state to carry workflow correlation.

A runtime command ID may remain metadata for durable correlation.

Do not force an application to interpret raw runtime IDs unless a current semantic consumer needs it.

Compare resume signatures including:

- `(State, Outcome) -> Decision`;
- `(State, Command, Outcome) -> Decision`;
- `(State, bytes, Outcome) -> Decision`;
- and another exact typed form.

Select the smallest form that supports deterministic replay, diagnostics, and command compatibility.

### 5.3 Imported host interfaces

An application declares zero or more exact imported interface slots.

Each slot must bind:

- one canonical local slot name;
- one exact interface identity;
- one exact request type;
- one exact outcome type;
- exact operation semantics;
- exact limits;
- and explicit optionality or requirement.

A slot is not authority.

An instance grant binding supplies authority.

A slot name is not interface identity.

A file path is not interface identity.

An adapter kind is not interface identity.

A request type alone is not authority.

### 5.4 Interface identity

Compare serious identity models:

- exact release and exported interface declaration identity;
- a dedicated immutable interface artifact;
- exact application-local interface declaration;
- a domain-separated digest over a canonical interface contract;
- and a closed built-in interface enum.

The leading bootstrap may use exact release/item identity when it preserves nominal equality and
avoids a second catalogue.

A dedicated interface artifact may win when adapters must bind independently of one application
release graph.

A closed built-in enum may win only if two interfaces remain simpler than generic metadata.

Do not introduce a global mutable interface registry.

Do not use human names or versions as exact binding.

### 5.5 Command routing

A command value must map deterministically to one imported interface.

Compare:

- one command sum variant per import slot;
- a command envelope containing an exact slot plus typed payload;
- one application-wide command type plus a static variant-to-slot map;
- and separate transition entries per interface.

The runtime must validate the mapping before publication.

A command cannot select an undeclared slot.

A command cannot smuggle a request type from another interface.

A command from another exact application cannot be replayed into this instance.

### 5.6 Outcomes

A host outcome is retained exact evidence.

It is not semantic state until a pure resume transition consumes it.

An outcome must bind:

- instance;
- application;
- state revision or pending command;
- command ID;
- interface;
- grant;
- adapter;
- outcome class;
- typed payload;
- evidence;
- and canonical digest where applicable.

The application-visible outcome type may omit infrastructure metadata.

The runtime must validate metadata and convert only compatible typed data to the resume entry.

Known success, known failure before visibility, unknown visibility, reconciliation results,
cancellation before action, timeout before action, timeout after possible visibility, and cleanup
failure remain distinguishable when applicable.

Do not reserve outcome variants that no retained adapter or exact lifecycle test can exercise.

### 5.7 Pure replay

Replay must:

- load exact embedded application meaning;
- validate every retained command and outcome;
- rerun every event transition;
- rerun every resume transition;
- reproduce exact state;
- reproduce exact response;
- reproduce exact pending command;
- reproduce exact command routing;
- reproduce exact outcome compatibility;
- and never execute a host adapter.

A cached compiled unit may accelerate replay.

It may not change the oracle.

### 5.8 Application tests

Application artifacts should carry exact tests for:

- every exported invocation profile;
- every stateful event entry;
- every resume entry;
- every imported interface command shape;
- every expected outcome class used by the application;
- completion;
- suspension;
- retry;
- cancellation;
- and terminal failure.

Pure application tests cannot call production adapters.

Use exact typed fake outcomes.

Skipped, unavailable, exhausted, or indeterminate cases do not pass.

## 6. Host interfaces, adapters, and grants

Host interfaces separate typed intent from authority and implementation.

Do not implement a dynamic plugin ecosystem in this campaign.

Built-in trusted adapters are sufficient to prove the model.

### 6.1 Activation interface migration

Migrate the current activation lifecycle into one exact host interface.

Its request vocabulary may include:

- validate exact application;
- activate exact application;
- inspect or reconcile exact activation.

Its outcome vocabulary may include:

- validation success;
- validation failure;
- activation success;
- failure before visibility;
- outcome unknown;
- reconciliation present;
- reconciliation absent;
- reconciliation indeterminate;
- cancellation before action;
- timeout before action;
- timeout after possible visibility;
- and cleanup failure.

Do not require unrelated applications to know these variants.

The activation grant remains narrow.

It may bind:

- exact instance;
- exact activation interface;
- production or fake adapter;
- one source namespace;
- one slot;
- path policy;
- size policy;
- and grant revision.

A production activation adapter remains trusted local code.

It is not a general filesystem API.

### 6.2 Exact immutable-blob interface

The leading second interface is a bounded immutable blob store.

Its purpose is to prove:

- payload-bearing typed commands;
- content identity distinct from application identity;
- idempotent create-if-absent publication;
- exact read or inspection;
- conflict rejection;
- unknown outcome;
- reconciliation;
- cross-instance authority denial;
- and reusable runtime routing.

Compare request shapes such as:

```text
BlobRequest =
    put { content: bytes }
  | inspect { digest: bytes }
  | read { digest: bytes }
```

Compare outcome shapes such as:

```text
BlobOutcome =
    stored { digest: bytes }
  | already_present { digest: bytes }
  | present { digest: bytes, content: bytes }
  | absent
  | conflict
  | known_failure
  | unknown
  | indeterminate
```

Do not retain every operation merely because it is listed.

Choose the smallest set needed by the representative application and reconciliation model.

The production grant should bind one exact private object namespace and independent byte/count
limits.

The adapter should publish immutable content under a derived digest without replacement.

A path remains a locator, not identity or authority.

Content digest equality is not provenance or authorization.

The adapter must validate canonical object placement on every read.

The fake adapter must be executor-disjoint from production.

### 6.3 Grant model

The current immutable grant digest may be insufficient for a long-lived generalized runtime.

Compare:

- immutable grant for instance lifetime;
- grant revision bound to every command;
- explicit grant replacement transition;
- new instance on grant change;
- and external named grant lookup.

Do not introduce mutable lookup authority by accident.

If grant rotation is not required by either retained application, preserve immutable grants and
record a precise future gate.

If grant revision is introduced, specify:

- identity;
- publication;
- attenuation;
- revocation;
- execution versus reconciliation authority;
- pending-command behavior;
- restart;
- audit;
- and unknown-outcome behavior.

### 6.4 Adapter registration

Compare:

- compile-time closed adapter table;
- explicit runtime-kernel construction with adapter objects;
- command-local adapter selection;
- signed dynamic plugins;
- and process-isolated adapter workers.

The leading bootstrap is a compile-time closed or explicitly constructed trusted adapter set.

Dynamic plugins lose unless a current external adapter consumer exists.

No plugin ABI, dynamic loading, or third-party native code enters merely to make future extension
look open.

### 6.5 Adapter lifecycle

Each retained adapter specifies:

- request validation;
- authority validation;
- resource reservation;
- attempt publication;
- visibility point;
- success evidence;
- known-failure point;
- unknown-outcome point;
- reconciliation;
- idempotency;
- cancellation;
- timeout;
- partial action;
- cleanup;
- crash recovery;
- result publication;
- inspection;
- and test oracle.

The runtime kernel routes.

The instance store owns durable command and outcome authority.

The adapter does not mutate semantic state.

## 7. Representative applications

The campaign must prove generality with complete applications.

### 7.1 Durable release controller

Migrate the existing durable release controller.

Preserve or improve its lifecycle:

- no active deployment;
- activation requested;
- validation pending;
- activation pending;
- active exact application;
- retryable failure;
- terminal failure;
- cancellation;
- unknown visibility;
- reconciliation;
- retry;
- and status inspection.

The application should import the activation interface.

It should not encode activation through magic integers and raw digest targets.

The runtime should not contain release-controller-specific state logic.

### 7.2 Durable exact blob publisher

Add a second application that imports the blob interface.

The leading workflow is:

- receive bounded bytes;
- validate business limits;
- record requested publication in typed state;
- issue one typed blob `put`;
- publish suspended state before host action;
- consume stored or already-present success;
- consume known failure;
- handle unknown visibility;
- issue typed inspection or reconciliation;
- reach success, retryable failure, cancellation, or terminal failure;
- and expose bounded status.

The application may transform or canonicalize bytes using pure lkjscript logic when useful.

Do not require a new hash primitive unless a current semantic consumer truly needs it.

The trusted adapter may derive the object digest as host evidence.

The application may retain that digest after resume.

### 7.3 Pure compute and loading control

Retain at least one pure application workload that stresses:

- repeated exact application loading;
- repeated lowering and verification;
- hot loops;
- calls;
- nominal construction;
- sum matching;
- byte operations;
- traps;
- and bounded memory.

Use it to separate runtime startup, compilation, and execution costs.

Do not make the benchmark itself a semantic feature.

### 7.4 Application independence

The two stateful applications must not share hidden controller code in their drivers.

They may share public library helpers for:

- command invocation;
- JSON encoding;
- process management;
- artifact construction;
- and evidence collection.

Their semantic state machines, interface types, and authority descriptors must remain independently
reviewable.

### 7.5 Public workflow requirements

Each application workflow must:

- use the supported public authoring surface;
- build exact releases;
- build the exact application;
- validate and test it;
- delete source workspaces;
- delete standalone release artifacts when the application embeds them;
- create at least two isolated instances;
- run across repeated process restart;
- exercise production and fake adapters;
- exercise duplicate event and outcome delivery;
- exercise stale base;
- exercise denied grant;
- exercise foreign interface;
- exercise corrupt retained bytes;
- exercise exact and one-over limits;
- exercise unknown response loss;
- reconcile where required;
- inspect bounded state and history;
- delete or tombstone according to the selected contract;
- and prove no identity reuse.

The application driver should emit a compact machine-readable evidence file.

The evidence file is observation, not semantic authority.

## 8. Runtime kernel

Introduce one topology-neutral runtime kernel.

Do not confuse it with the workspace `Engine`.

The runtime kernel should own operational composition of existing exact owners.

### 8.1 Responsibilities

Candidate responsibilities are:

- exact application loader;
- validated application registry for one process;
- derived compiled-unit cache;
- exact instance-store opener;
- transition preparer;
- resource admission;
- per-instance serialization;
- cross-instance dispatch;
- grant descriptor validation;
- adapter routing;
- host execution coordination;
- stage telemetry;
- bounded inspection;
- and orderly shutdown.

Retain only responsibilities with a current consumer.

### 8.2 Non-responsibilities

The runtime kernel must not become:

- workspace semantic authority;
- release resolver;
- mutable package registry;
- hidden application store;
- hidden current instance;
- hidden grant registry without an exact contract;
- semantic scheduler;
- durable queue by accident;
- source of wall-clock semantic values;
- source of randomness without a capability;
- secret store;
- general filesystem service;
- plugin manager;
- or sandbox claim.

### 8.3 Construction

Prefer explicit construction.

The runtime kernel should receive:

- deployment policy;
- state and instance roots;
- adapter set;
- cache policy;
- resource limits;
- telemetry sink;
- and exact platform assumptions.

Avoid process-global mutable singletons.

Avoid ambient environment reads in semantic operation.

If environment or configuration files select deployment policy, validate them before runtime
construction and keep them outside semantic artifacts.

### 8.4 Request lifecycle

A runtime request should have explicit phases:

1. decode and validate request envelope;
2. authenticate topology peer where applicable;
3. resolve exact operation and authority;
4. reserve operational resources;
5. open or reuse exact validated application state;
6. open or reuse exact instance authority;
7. serialize the target instance;
8. prepare the pure transition or host operation;
9. preflight bounded response;
10. publish semantic or host authority;
11. encode the receipt;
12. release reservations;
13. update disposable observations;
14. return or report output failure.

A failure in one phase must not be mislabeled as another.

A response write failure does not roll back published authority.

Exact idempotency remains the retry route.

### 8.5 Reuse boundaries

The kernel may reuse:

- decoded immutable application artifacts;
- validated release graphs;
- flattened exact graphs;
- verified Core IR;
- compiled functions;
- immutable layout tables;
- interface metadata;
- adapter metadata;
- and read-only inspection summaries.

It must not reuse stale:

- mutable HEAD;
- instance revision;
- grant revision;
- pending command;
- outcome state;
- resource reservation;
- caller identity;
- or authorization decision.

Every reuse category requires an invalidation rule.

### 8.6 Locking

Compare:

- current store-wide lock;
- per-instance locks plus store metadata lock;
- process-internal actor ownership;
- file locks per instance;
- and a single runtime serialization loop.

Retain the simplest model that permits the representative concurrency workload.

Per-instance serial order is mandatory.

Do not weaken cross-process exclusion while a one-shot topology remains supported.

If the runtime session becomes the sole writer topology, specify stale process recovery and
single-owner acquisition.

Use a written lock order.

Test lock-order violations and shutdown races.

### 8.7 Shutdown

Shutdown must define:

- stop accepting work;
- reject or drain queued work;
- cancellation point before action;
- treatment of active pure transitions;
- treatment of active host operations;
- response flushing;
- cache disposal;
- telemetry flushing;
- lock release;
- and restart recovery.

Do not claim graceful cancellation after possible host visibility unless the adapter proves it.

A forced process kill must leave durable authority in a valid, unknown, or corrupt-rejected state.

## 9. Runtime topology decision

The runtime kernel is mandatory.

A resident supervisor is not predetermined.

### 9.1 One-shot topology

Measure the current one-process-per-command topology.

Its strengths include:

- simple lifecycle;
- natural process cleanup;
- no stale resident state;
- easy shell composition;
- and low operational trust.

Its costs may include:

- repeated startup;
- repeated application validation;
- repeated lowering;
- repeated instance replay;
- repeated schema/help discovery;
- and process explosion in complete workflows.

Do not remove one-shot adapters before the common kernel exists.

A one-shot CLI may remain as a thin adapter even if a session wins.

It must not duplicate semantic implementation.

### 9.2 Foreground session

Prototype a foreground line-delimited session around the runtime kernel.

The caller owns startup and shutdown.

The session may retain:

- application cache;
- compiled-unit cache;
- opened read-only metadata;
- adapter instances;
- resource accounting;
- and stage counters.

Each request keeps an independent publication boundary.

Request IDs must permit exact correlation.

Malformed input must not desynchronize the stream.

Output failure must have an explicit process result.

EOF behavior must be explicit.

A session must not create an implicit current application or instance.

### 9.3 Unix-domain-socket supervisor

Prototype only after the foreground session is stable.

Specify:

- socket path policy;
- ownership and permissions;
- peer credential use;
- protocol version;
- request framing;
- maximum frame size;
- connection limits;
- idle limits;
- request multiplexing;
- output backpressure;
- authority mapping;
- stale socket recovery;
- singleton ownership;
- startup race;
- shutdown;
- upgrade;
- and observability.

Peer UID is deployment evidence, not semantic capability identity.

A socket process boundary is not isolation from trusted adapter code.

### 9.4 Automatic startup

Do not add implicit auto-spawn unless a retained consumer proves that explicit lifecycle is the main
cost.

Auto-spawn requires:

- race-free singleton creation;
- exact binary selection;
- environment and configuration binding;
- version agreement;
- stale process replacement;
- log destination;
- failure reporting;
- and no silent use of an unintended supervisor.

A foreground session is preferable when it provides most amortization without these obligations.

### 9.5 External service manager

System service integration may be documented or prototyped as deployment evidence.

Do not make systemd, launchd, containers, or another manager semantic authority.

A service manager can strengthen process lifecycle and OS resource control.

It does not replace application grants, runtime accounting, or replay.

Do not add packaging or install scripts without a current installation consumer.

### 9.6 Retention gate

Before measuring, record the threshold for retaining a resident supervisor.

The threshold should combine:

- complete warm workload speedup;
- process reduction;
- compile and validation reuse;
- aggregate resource need;
- concurrent instance need;
- memory cost;
- lifecycle complexity;
- failure quality;
- debugging cost;
- and security surface.

A resident topology must improve the complete product workflow, not merely an empty ping.

If benefit is marginal, retain the foreground session or one-shot kernel and delete the socket
prototype.

If benefit is substantial, cut over coherently and delete duplicated topology code.

Record the reversal condition.

## 10. Scheduling and concurrency

Scheduling is an operational policy.

It is not hidden language semantics.

### 10.1 Baseline ordering

Preserve:

- exact serial order per instance;
- exact base revision;
- exact event-key deduplication;
- state publication before host execution;
- one immutable host outcome;
- and pure resume.

Cross-instance work may be independent.

### 10.2 Transition-boundary scheduling

The leading bootstrap schedules at request or transition boundaries.

A pure transition runs to:

- completion;
- semantic suspension;
- deterministic fuel exhaustion;
- deterministic frame exhaustion;
- resource rejection;
- or infrastructure failure.

Do not preempt in the middle merely to claim a scheduler.

Transition-boundary scheduling can still provide:

- bounded queueing;
- per-instance serialization;
- cross-instance fairness;
- compilation concurrency limits;
- host-operation concurrency limits;
- priority classes;
- and overload behavior.

### 10.3 Mid-transition suspension

A future resumable interpreter may support operational time slicing.

Do not persist an opaque continuation in this campaign unless a current workload proves it is
necessary.

If prototyped, compare:

- explicit interpreter frame snapshot;
- bounded cooperative yield checks;
- deterministic fuel slices;
- epoch or wall-time interruption;
- and process-level termination.

Specify whether interruption is:

- semantic trap;
- retryable infrastructure result;
- transparent operational yield;
- or cancellation input.

Transparent yield must resume exact values, traps, fuel, memory accounting, and diagnostics.

Do not let scheduler slice count become observable accidentally.

### 10.4 Fairness

Compare fairness policies:

- FIFO;
- round robin by request;
- weighted round robin by instance;
- deficit accounting by deterministic fuel;
- fixed priority;
- aging;
- and no retained scheduler.

Do not use wall-clock runtime as deterministic semantic fuel.

A heavy application must not monopolize all runtime capacity indefinitely.

A low-priority application must not starve without an explicit policy.

### 10.5 Host operations

Host operations may block independently of pure execution.

Separate limits for:

- active pure transitions;
- active compilations;
- active host operations;
- and queued resumes.

Do not hold a global semantic lock while an external operation blocks unless the current serial model
requires it and measurement justifies it.

Never permit a second command for one suspended instance unless the contract defines it.

### 10.6 Threading

Compare:

- single-threaded runtime loop;
- one bounded pure-execution pool;
- separate bounded adapter pool;
- per-instance actors;
- and no internal pool.

Do not add an async dependency automatically.

The standard library may be sufficient for the bootstrap.

Every retained thread requires:

- ownership;
- shutdown;
- panic behavior;
- queue bound;
- backpressure;
- name;
- metrics;
- and test control.

A thread panic must not silently acknowledge success.

### 10.7 Determinism

Cross-instance completion order may be operationally nondeterministic.

Each instance's accepted history remains deterministic.

Inspection must not infer semantic order from process completion order.

Testing must control or record scheduler decisions when they affect operational assertions.

The deterministic fake scheduler, if retained, is test infrastructure rather than semantic
authority.

## 11. Resource governance

Resource governance must work without a resident service.

A resident service may make aggregate enforcement more effective.

### 11.1 Policy layers

Separate:

- semantic execution policy;
- application artifact policy;
- instance policy;
- grant policy;
- runtime deployment policy;
- adapter policy;
- cache policy;
- scheduler policy;
- and OS containment.

Do not copy one limit into every layer without an owner.

The effective limit is explicit.

The most restrictive applicable authority may win only when the rule is specified and inspectable.

### 11.2 Reservation

Reserve before work when possible.

Examples:

- reserve output bound before publication;
- reserve queue slot before accepting work;
- reserve compilation slot before lowering;
- reserve cache insertion bytes before retaining a unit;
- reserve adapter operation before attempt publication;
- reserve temporary disk estimate before writing;
- and reserve state/history allowance before transition publication.

Reservation failure must not partially publish semantic state unless the accepted transition already
made that resource commitment explicit.

### 11.3 Release

Every reservation has one release path.

Cover:

- success;
- semantic rejection;
- trap;
- cancellation before action;
- timeout before action;
- unknown host outcome;
- response write failure;
- worker panic;
- connection drop;
- session EOF;
- supervisor shutdown;
- and process crash.

Durable retained bytes remain accounted after process exit.

Disposable memory and queue reservations do not.

### 11.4 Memory

Keep distinct:

- logical value bytes;
- public materialized bytes;
- managed visible bytes;
- retained backing bytes;
- managed object count;
- application artifact bytes;
- decoded graph bytes;
- Core IR bytes;
- compiled-unit bytes;
- cache bytes;
- state bytes;
- history bytes;
- adapter buffers;
- response buffers;
- and observed process RSS.

Do not add incomparable categories.

Do not double-count one backing allocation as several exact limits unless the policy deliberately
does so.

### 11.5 Disk

Track applicable:

- workspace authority bytes;
- release artifact bytes;
- application artifact bytes;
- instance records;
- outcomes;
- attempts;
- HEAD;
- tombstones;
- temporary publication files;
- blob objects;
- compiled cache;
- profiles;
- logs;
- and evidence.

Caches, logs, and profiles require bounded retention.

Do not make cleanup of disposable data part of semantic correctness.

### 11.6 CPU and work

Keep distinct:

- semantic fuel;
- validation work;
- replay work;
- lowering work;
- verifier work;
- compilation work;
- adapter preparation;
- host blocking time;
- queue wait;
- and wall time.

A wall-time timeout cannot substitute for deterministic fuel.

A deployment CPU quota cannot substitute for semantic work limits.

### 11.7 Overload

Define exact behavior for:

- queue full;
- compilation slots full;
- adapter slots full;
- cache reservation failure;
- memory pressure;
- disk preflight failure;
- open-file limit;
- and supervisor connection limit.

Classify each as:

- deterministic policy rejection;
- retryable operational busy;
- permanent authority denial;
- resource exhaustion;
- or unknown outcome.

Never silently drop an accepted mutation.

### 11.8 Inspection

Bounded runtime inspection should expose:

- policy identities;
- current reservations;
- queue counts;
- active operation counts;
- cache counts and bytes;
- hit and miss counts;
- evictions;
- compile count;
- loaded application count;
- adapter operation count;
- rejected admissions;
- and explicit omissions.

Inspection is operational observation.

It does not become semantic authority.

## 12. Stage telemetry and benchmark design

Do not optimize an unmeasured aggregate.

### 12.1 Measurement record

Every retained measurement records:

- exact commit;
- dirty state;
- host OS and kernel;
- architecture;
- CPU model;
- logical CPU count;
- memory;
- filesystem;
- Rust toolchain;
- Cargo lock digest;
- build profile;
- command;
- environment changes;
- input artifact digests;
- sample count;
- warm-up;
- cache state;
- topology;
- limits;
- result;
- failures;
- and unavailable metrics.

Do not mix cold and warm samples.

Do not report one sample as a distribution.

### 12.2 Stage boundaries

Use monotonic time for operational measurement.

Do not expose monotonic timestamps in semantic data.

Keep counters for exact work where useful:

- bytes read;
- items decoded;
- graph nodes;
- graph edges;
- functions lowered;
- Core instructions verified;
- execution fuel;
- frames;
- allocations;
- copies;
- records replayed;
- history bytes;
- adapter bytes;
- and output bytes.

Prefer exact counters over noisy time when diagnosing a stage.

### 12.3 Workload classes

Measure:

- cold one-shot pure invocation;
- warm repeated pure invocation;
- cold stateful event;
- warm repeated stateful event;
- cold resume;
- warm resume;
- restart replay;
- ten instances;
- one hundred instances when bounded;
- concurrent independent instances;
- denied authority;
- unknown outcome;
- corruption rejection;
- cache eviction;
- and shutdown under load.

### 12.4 Compile versus execute

Separate:

- artifact validation;
- graph flattening;
- lowering;
- Core verification;
- execution;
- and result materialization.

A JIT does not solve artifact validation.

A daemon does not solve slow semantic execution by itself.

A cache does not solve a cold unique application.

A bytecode format does not solve host blocking.

Select the measured term.

### 12.5 Thresholds

State retention thresholds before seeing final results.

A threshold may combine:

- absolute latency;
- relative improvement;
- process count;
- memory;
- binary size;
- clean build;
- incremental build;
- verification surface;
- failure quality;
- and operator steps.

Do not lower the threshold after a favored prototype underperforms.

Record a stop rule for every prototype.

## 13. Derived caches and compiled execution

The campaign must establish a disciplined execution-tier path.

It need not retain a JIT.

### 13.1 Application load cache

The smallest likely useful cache stores an independently validated immutable application load
result.

Candidate contents:

- canonical application bytes;
- exact digest;
- decoded profile;
- exact release graph;
- flattened nominal map;
- verified tests result under exact artifact;
- and interface metadata.

Do not cache a current test pass across a changed test policy or compiler contract without exact
binding.

### 13.2 Core cache

A verified Core cache may store:

- selected entry closure;
- exact type tables;
- layouts;
- Core IR;
- verifier result;
- origin map;
- and runtime representation contract.

Its key must bind exact application and entry.

If policy changes lowering or representation, bind policy.

If policy only changes fuel, do not duplicate units unnecessarily.

Prove the distinction.

### 13.3 Persistent cache

A persistent cache adds:

- hostile decode;
- versioning;
- target binding;
- corruption handling;
- atomic publication;
- eviction;
- disk accounting;
- cleanup;
- and supply-chain surface.

Retain it only when process restart destroys a material warm benefit.

A foreground session may make an in-memory cache sufficient.

### 13.4 Bytecode

Compare bytecode only after measuring interpreter dispatch and Core representation.

A retained bytecode needs:

- exact semantics;
- verifier;
- version;
- target independence or binding;
- bounds;
- branch encoding;
- call encoding;
- nominal layout binding;
- trap mapping;
- source diagnostics;
- and differential tests.

Do not make bytecode distribution authority merely to cache it.

### 13.5 Baseline native compiler

A baseline compiler may trade compile speed for execution speed.

Compare:

- direct machine-code emission;
- Cranelift or another backend;
- C or LLVM emission;
- threaded interpreter;
- and no native tier.

A new backend dependency must justify build, binary, audit, platform, and maintenance cost.

No backend may bypass exact Core verification.

### 13.6 Optimizing tier

An optimizing tier is not the default next step.

It requires:

- a current hot workload;
- profile or static trigger;
- exact tier identity;
- compilation budget;
- code cache budget;
- invalidation;
- deoptimization or no-deoptimization contract;
- trap equivalence;
- resource-accounting equivalence;
- and differential stress tests.

Do not copy V8 tier count or architecture.

Use external systems only to identify tradeoffs.

### 13.7 Tier selection

Tier choice must not alter semantic results.

A runtime may choose tiers based on:

- exact invocation count;
- deterministic work count;
- measured execution time;
- code size;
- compile budget;
- queue pressure;
- and deployment policy.

If wall-time hotness affects only optimization, it remains operational.

Profiles must not contain secrets or unbounded raw inputs.

### 13.8 Native-code trust

Generated code requires:

- writable-versus-executable memory policy;
- instruction-cache handling;
- architecture validation;
- feature validation;
- code bounds;
- call ABI;
- trap bridge;
- signal or exception model;
- stack bounds;
- and process crash behavior.

A worker may isolate generated-code failure operationally.

A worker is not automatically a hostile-code sandbox.

Do not overclaim.

### 13.9 Reversal

For every retained tier record:

- workload that justified it;
- baseline;
- absolute benefit;
- relative benefit;
- compile cost;
- memory cost;
- build cost;
- binary cost;
- verification cost;
- platform scope;
- oracle;
- and deletion condition.

Delete a tier that no longer pays rent.

## 14. Agent-facing surface and provider cost

The system exists primarily for coding agents.

The runtime campaign must improve agent operation, not only runtime speed.

### 14.1 Compact orientation

Provide one compact runtime orientation result containing:

- public contract versions;
- schema or interface digests;
- supported topologies;
- supported adapters;
- supported exact roots;
- key limits;
- and exact commands for expansion.

Do not dump every interface, schema, and help page by default.

### 14.2 Task-scoped expansion

Permit exact expansion for:

- one application world;
- one interface;
- one grant shape;
- one instance;
- one pending command;
- one outcome;
- one runtime policy;
- one cache policy;
- and one diagnostic code.

Bound every expansion.

Use digest-based `unchanged` responses where reconstruction is deterministic and useful.

### 14.3 Apply and refresh

Measure whether an apply receipt plus compact exact delta reduces a second context call.

Compare:

- current compact receipt plus context rebuild;
- receipt plus exact changed targets;
- receipt plus bounded new context packet;
- and no change.

Do not add response-preflight complexity without measured task benefit.

### 14.4 Interface authoring

An agent should not need to edit raw magic tags.

Provide one exact authoring route for:

- nominal request types;
- nominal outcome types;
- complete/suspended decision type;
- imported interface slot;
- event transition;
- resume transition;
- application cases;
- instance grant binding;
- and fake outcome.

The authoring surface may be the existing editable semantic document when extended cleanly.

Do not create a second source language.

### 14.5 Diagnosis

Runtime and instance diagnostics should identify:

- exact application;
- exact instance;
- exact revision;
- exact pending command;
- exact interface;
- exact grant;
- exact adapter;
- exact stage;
- stable error code;
- bounded target;
- legal next actions;
- and omitted details.

Do not require reading Rust source to distinguish semantic rejection from operational busy or unknown
host visibility.

### 14.6 Frozen agent tasks

Freeze deterministic tasks before changing the surface.

Include tasks to:

- create an interface;
- add an imported slot;
- migrate an application;
- create a blob-publisher application;
- bind a grant;
- diagnose denied authority;
- diagnose unknown outcome;
- reconcile;
- repair a malformed outcome type;
- inspect resource exhaustion;
- inspect cache behavior;
- and review an incompatible format cutover.

Validators decide success.

Model judgment does not decide correctness.

### 14.7 Provider telemetry

When the harness exposes telemetry, record:

- model identity;
- attempt count;
- input tokens;
- cached input tokens;
- output tokens;
- reasoning tokens;
- tool calls;
- wall time;
- price schedule;
- and exact cost.

When telemetry is absent, record only available bytes, calls, processes, files, and elapsed time.

Never estimate token cost from UTF-8 bytes.

### 14.8 Prompt retention

Keep this campaign prompt only while it has a current consumer.

After completion, Git history remains the archive.

Do not accumulate several superseded 300-KiB prompts in ordinary discovery.

Keep root `AGENTS.md` within its durable size budget.

## 15. Artifact, instance, and protocol cutover

The generalized world will likely require incompatible format changes.

Use direct cutover.

### 15.1 Application format

A new application artifact should encode applicable:

- exact release graph;
- exact world exports;
- exact imported interfaces;
- exact stateful type bindings;
- exact decision mapping;
- exact command routing;
- exact tests;
- exact run policy;
- limits;
- and canonical digest.

Do not retain application format 3 as a fallback.

Reject it directly after cutover.

### 15.2 Instance format

A new instance format should encode applicable:

- exact new application bytes;
- state records;
- typed response;
- typed pending command;
- exact interface binding;
- exact grant binding;
- attempts;
- typed outcomes;
- event receipts;
- resume receipts;
- HEAD;
- and tombstone.

Do not reinterpret old activation command bytes under the new model.

Reject old successful instance formats directly.

### 15.3 Release format

Do not bump release format merely because the application world changes.

Bump it only if reusable releases must represent exported interface declarations or another changed
semantic fact.

Compare keeping interface metadata in the application versus making it reusable release content.

Select one owner.

### 15.4 Workspace semantic schema

If new semantic declaration kinds or operations are unnecessary, do not bump workspace schema.

If exact interface declarations become reusable semantic items, update the schema once and delete
old successful forms.

Do not maintain parallel declarations and application-local metadata for the same fact.

### 15.5 Protocol and command contracts

Keep workspace RPC separate from command-local application, instance, and runtime contracts unless a
measured consumer proves unification.

A runtime session protocol may need its own exact version.

Do not add runtime records to the global workspace schema merely for discoverability.

Provide compact command-local contract inspection.

### 15.6 Canonical encoding

Every new public binary or JSON boundary requires:

- magic or explicit version;
- domain binding;
- checked lengths;
- checked counts;
- canonical ordering;
- duplicate rejection;
- unknown-field rejection;
- wrong-domain rejection;
- digest verification;
- re-encoding equality;
- truncation rejection;
- trailing rejection;
- exact limits;
- and mutation tests.

### 15.7 Publication

Prepared responses must satisfy output limits before durable publication when a response is part of
the publication contract.

Known-before-visibility and unknown-after-visibility remain explicit.

No automatic retry of an unknown operation.

Exact idempotency keys and inspection are the reconciliation route.

## 16. Persistence, replay, and crash semantics

Preserve the strongest properties of the current instance vertical.

### 16.1 State publication

The leading order remains:

1. validate exact request and authority;
2. evaluate pure transition;
3. validate next state, response, and typed command;
4. preflight receipt;
5. publish immutable next-state record;
6. publish HEAD;
7. return transition receipt;
8. separately validate grant;
9. publish attempt marker where applicable;
10. execute adapter;
11. publish immutable typed outcome;
12. return host receipt;
13. accept a later pure resume.

Change this order only with a stronger proved contract.

### 16.2 Full records versus journal

Current histories are small.

Do not introduce a database or compact journal by default.

Measure the two new applications.

Compare:

- full state per revision;
- checkpoint plus delta;
- append-only transition journal;
- content-addressed state objects;
- and current behavior.

A replacement must preserve replay, event-key behavior, unresolved outcomes, corruption rejection,
and recovery.

### 16.3 Generic command storage

Store canonical typed command values.

Bind them to exact application, state revision, interface, and grant.

Do not store only an adapter-private byte blob when deterministic replay needs application-visible
shape.

Adapter-private evidence may remain separately bounded.

### 16.4 Generic outcome storage

Store canonical typed outcome values plus infrastructure class.

A recomputed valid envelope with a command-incompatible payload must reject.

A foreign interface outcome must reject.

A foreign grant outcome must reject.

A fake outcome must not enter a production adapter domain.

### 16.5 Attempt markers

An attempt marker must be published before any action whose visibility could become unknown.

An idempotent content-addressed put may have a simpler retry contract.

Do not assume idempotency without proving same-content and conflict behavior.

The marker should bind exact command and adapter.

After a crash with a marker but no outcome, recovery must not guess.

### 16.6 Runtime queues

Runtime request queues are disposable by default.

Do not acknowledge durable acceptance merely because a request entered memory.

A request becomes durable only at its owning publication boundary.

If unattended durable jobs become a requirement, specify a separate durable job authority.

Do not smuggle it into a supervisor queue.

### 16.7 Startup

Startup validates:

- deployment policy;
- runtime state root;
- adapter descriptors;
- caches as disposable hostile data;
- instance store structure;
- and exact supported contract versions.

A corrupt cache may be discarded.

Corrupt semantic or instance authority must reject.

Do not silently rebuild or skip a corrupt retained record.

### 16.8 Crash testing

Enumerate every write, sync, link, rename, visibility, cleanup, response, IPC, and shutdown boundary.

Classify each injected failure as:

- known no change;
- known complete;
- explicit unknown;
- retryable busy;
- or corruption rejection.

No other hand-waved class is acceptable.

## 17. Security and isolation

The generalized runtime expands attack surface.

Treat every new boundary explicitly.

### 17.1 Threat actors

Consider separately:

- malformed model-authored request;
- malicious local unprivileged client;
- compromised application artifact;
- corrupt instance store;
- corrupt cache;
- hostile adapter descriptor;
- symlink and path substitution;
- concurrent local administrator;
- compromised dependency;
- generated-code bug;
- runaway application;
- and hostile OS administrator.

Do not claim protection against actors inside the TCB.

### 17.2 Capability confinement

A grant must be narrower than a general filesystem or process capability.

The activation adapter should touch only its exact source namespace and slot.

The blob adapter should touch only its exact object namespace.

Interface mismatch rejects before host action.

Grant mismatch rejects before attempt publication unless the attempt itself is relevant evidence.

Paths remain locators.

### 17.3 IPC

If a supervisor socket is retained:

- use private permissions;
- validate peer credentials where available;
- bind deployment authorization explicitly;
- bound connections and frames;
- reject protocol downgrade;
- reject request confusion;
- prevent terminal or log injection;
- and define output backpressure.

Do not treat peer credentials as application grants.

### 17.4 Multi-user scope

The current bootstrap is local single-user.

Do not claim multi-user authorization.

A shared supervisor used by several OS users requires a new threat model and authority system.

Reject or defer that topology rather than documenting aspirational isolation.

### 17.5 Generated code

If native code is retained:

- keep semantic input untrusted;
- keep verifier boundaries explicit;
- bind target features;
- bound code memory;
- prevent writable and executable overlap where platform permits;
- map traps safely;
- test corrupt cache bytes;
- and state residual trust.

Do not claim formal memory safety of generated code without proof.

### 17.6 OS resource controls

Linux cgroups or a service manager may enforce deployment CPU, memory, task, and I/O limits.

These controls may be useful for a retained supervisor or worker.

They are deployment policy, not semantic limits.

Do not require privileged setup for ordinary local authoring unless the benefit is decisive.

### 17.7 Secrets

Neither representative adapter requires secrets.

Do not add a secret store.

Ensure application artifacts, state, command values, fake outcomes, diagnostics, profiles, and
evidence do not accidentally capture ambient environment.

### 17.8 Supply chain

Audit every new dependency for:

- purpose;
- transitive count;
- unsafe code exposure;
- build script;
- platform code;
- license;
- maintenance;
- binary impact;
- build impact;
- and replacement difficulty.

A JIT backend or async runtime is not free merely because it is popular.

## 18. Architecture compression and source ownership

Generalization can reduce or increase complexity.

Measure both.

### 18.1 Remove activation coupling

Delete activation-specific facts from generic layers.

Expected deletion candidates include:

- integer command tags in application profile;
- fixed target bytes;
- fixed resume `i64, bytes` contract;
- activation outcome tags in generic application code;
- activation grant assumptions in instance-generic code;
- activation-specific CLI branches that can become interface operations;
- and duplicated fake-outcome validation.

Retain activation semantics only inside its interface and adapter owners.

### 18.2 Keep one interface owner

Do not create:

- Rust interface enum;
- JSON schema;
- application metadata table;
- documentation table;
- CLI help table;
- and test table

as independent mutable owners of the same facts.

Select one executable owner.

Derive projections where useful.

Keep cross-field semantic validation explicit.

### 18.3 Runtime modules

Organize around stable ownership, not aspirational layering.

Candidate modules may include:

- `world`;
- `interface`;
- `grant`;
- `runtime`;
- `scheduler`;
- `resource`;
- `cache`;
- `adapter`;
- `adapter/activation`;
- `adapter/blob`;
- and `runtime_protocol`.

Do not create empty forwarding modules.

Do not split one coherent owner merely to satisfy a line target.

### 18.4 Existing large modules

For each affected large module, decide:

- retain cohesive owner;
- extract exact independent owner;
- derive duplicated facts;
- delete obsolete branch;
- or rewrite around the new domain.

Measure source opened for representative changes before and after.

Do not stage a repository-wide cosmetic split during the semantic cutover.

### 18.5 Contract catalogue

The global workspace contract is large.

The command-local pattern already avoided adding release, application, and instance DTOs to it.

Continue command-local runtime/interface contracts unless repeated equal changes prove a stronger
shared derivation.

A proc macro or IDL loses if expanded output becomes another source of truth.

### 18.6 Example drivers

Current Python example drivers are large.

Audit whether repeated process orchestration, JSON validation, evidence collection, and corruption
helpers can share one test-support package.

Do not hide application-specific assertions in a generic helper.

Keep public examples understandable as product workflows.

### 18.7 Generated tests

Generated invariant tests are acceptable only when:

- generator is deterministic;
- generated cases have an executable owner;
- failures identify the exact case;
- regeneration cannot silently drift;
- and retained source cost is justified.

Delete stale generated cases tied to old formats.

### 18.8 Prompt cleanup

After selecting this campaign, the previous active prompt has no current authority.

Before final handoff, remove superseded tracked prompts without a named consumer.

Do not delete this active prompt during the same campaign unless the active user task explicitly
requests a post-campaign cleanup commit.

## 19. Serious architecture alternatives

Do not treat the leading design as predetermined.

Compare at least the following serious alternatives.

### 19.1 Preserve activation-specific stateful ABI

Strength:

- smallest immediate change.

Weakness:

- cannot express unrelated host operations cleanly.

It wins only if the second useful application proves that no host interface generalization is
needed.

### 19.2 Generic byte command and byte outcome

Strength:

- small runtime shape.

Weakness:

- moves typing into application conventions or adapters.

It wins only if canonical schemas preserve exact typing without duplicate owners and agents perform
better.

### 19.3 Application-owned nominal command and outcome types

Strength:

- reuses current language products and sums;
- keeps workflow types local and exact.

Weakness:

- runtime needs exact routing metadata.

This is the leading hypothesis.

### 19.4 Dedicated interface artifacts

Strength:

- reusable interface identity independent of one application.

Weakness:

- adds a new artifact and identity domain.

It wins only if two adapters and applications demonstrate real reuse that release/item identity
cannot express cleanly.

### 19.5 Built-in closed interface enum

Strength:

- simple bootstrap validation.

Weakness:

- risks moving application-specific vocabulary into the runtime.

It may win when exactly two narrow built-ins remain simpler and the extension gate is explicit.

### 19.6 Algebraic effects and handlers

Strength:

- typed effect signatures and composition.

Weakness:

- does not by itself solve grants, crash publication, unknown visibility, replay, or adapter
  lifecycle.

Do not implement broadly for two interfaces unless it reduces total semantics.

### 19.7 Direct host calls from the interpreter

Strength:

- concise application code.

Weakness:

- pure execution becomes host-dependent;
- authority and resource lifetime enter the interpreter.

It is disfavored.

### 19.8 External Rust or Python orchestrator

Strength:

- easy prototype.

Weakness:

- application no longer owns workflow semantics.

Retain only as driver and oracle.

### 19.9 General filesystem capability

Strength:

- can implement activation and blob storage.

Weakness:

- authority is far broader;
- application semantics become path-oriented;
- unknown partial behavior multiplies.

It loses unless several current interfaces prove a safe shared attenuated abstraction.

### 19.10 Workflow engine or database

Strength:

- durable jobs, timers, transactions, and replay services.

Weakness:

- large TCB and operational scope.

It loses while current histories and workflows remain small and synchronous.

### 19.11 Actor runtime

Strength:

- natural per-instance serialization and messaging.

Weakness:

- mailbox durability, supervision, scheduling, and distributed identity can become accidental
  semantics.

Use actor-like internal ownership only if it simplifies the selected runtime.

### 19.12 WASI component runtime

Strength:

- mature typed import/export and capability ecosystem.

Weakness:

- new runtime, ABI, toolchain, and semantic translation.

It may inform interface design.

Do not embed it without a current interoperability consumer.

### 19.13 Resident Unix-socket supervisor

Strength:

- cache reuse, centralized admission, multi-client operation.

Weakness:

- lifecycle, IPC, authentication, stale state, and debugging.

It is evidence-gated.

### 19.14 Foreground runtime session

Strength:

- amortization with caller-owned lifecycle.

Weakness:

- one stream, limited multi-client operation.

It is the leading minimum long-lived topology.

### 19.15 No long-lived topology

Strength:

- lowest operational complexity.

Weakness:

- repeated startup and no aggregate coordination.

It remains a valid winner.

### 19.16 Persistent compiled cache without supervisor

Strength:

- warm restart benefit with one-shot commands.

Weakness:

- hostile cache format and disk lifecycle.

Compare after in-memory session caching.

### 19.17 Baseline native compiler

Strength:

- execution speed with lower compile complexity than optimizing JIT.

Weakness:

- backend, executable memory, platform, and differential surface.

It is evidence-gated.

### 19.18 Optimizing JIT

Strength:

- peak hot-code performance.

Weakness:

- highest complexity and delayed payoff.

It loses without a representative hot application and stable lower tiers.

### 19.19 Process-isolated adapters

Strength:

- crash and descriptor boundary.

Weakness:

- IPC, worker lifecycle, result ambiguity, and no automatic sandbox.

It wins only for untrusted or blocking adapters that current built-ins do not create.

### 19.20 Separate deployment manager

Strength:

- operational policy and OS limits.

Weakness:

- additional service and state.

It is outside core semantics unless a retained deployment consumer exists.

## 20. Milestone sequence

Use milestones that each leave a coherent verified state.

Do not create a broad half-integrated architecture.

### Milestone 0: audit and frozen evidence

Deliver:

- checkout ledger;
- active instruction digest;
- current public workflow results;
- current source and binary measurements;
- current stage timing instrumentation plan;
- frozen application and agent tasks;
- architecture alternatives;
- stop rules;
- and reversal gates.

Do not change public formats yet.

### Milestone 1: interface model prototype

Build the smallest in-memory or test-only prototype that can express:

- activation request and outcome;
- blob request and outcome;
- completed decision;
- suspended decision;
- exact routing;
- exact grant binding;
- and pure resume.

Compare the alternatives.

Select one.

Delete losing prototype code.

### Milestone 2: semantic and artifact cutover

Implement the selected world contract through:

- semantic owner;
- application build;
- canonical artifact;
- inspection;
- tests;
- typed invocation;
- and strict rejection.

Bump only affected formats.

Delete old application format support.

### Milestone 3: generic instance cutover

Implement through:

- creation;
- event validation;
- event publication;
- typed pending command;
- generic attempt;
- typed outcome;
- resume;
- replay;
- history;
- inspection;
- deletion;
- and corruption tests.

Delete old instance support.

### Milestone 4: adapters and applications

Migrate activation.

Add blob interface and adapter.

Migrate durable controller.

Add durable blob publisher.

Run full public lifecycles.

### Milestone 5: runtime kernel

Route one-shot commands through one runtime kernel.

Add explicit resource admission and stage counters.

Prove no semantic duplication.

### Milestone 6: foreground session

Add and measure the foreground session.

Retain or delete based on the predeclared gate.

### Milestone 7: resident supervisor experiment

Only if unresolved product needs remain.

Prototype, measure, retain or delete.

Do not leave an unsupported socket path.

### Milestone 8: cache and execution-tier experiment

Instrument cold and warm stages.

Prototype the smallest measured acceleration.

Retain at most one new production acceleration plus the oracle.

Delete losing tiers and cache formats.

### Milestone 9: agent economy

Run frozen deterministic tasks.

Compare context and correction behavior.

Retain one authoring and diagnosis surface.

Delete losing convenience routes.

### Milestone 10: architecture compression and handoff

Delete stale formats, prompts, docs, tests, commands, modules, and prototypes.

Update every fact owner.

Run all checks and workflows.

Record exact evidence and remaining gates.

## 21. Stop rules

Stop speculative expansion when any of these conditions holds.

- Two narrow built-in interfaces remain simpler than a generic interface artifact.
- A dedicated interface registry has no second independent consumer.
- A dynamic plugin ABI has no external adapter.
- A resident supervisor does not materially improve complete workflows.
- A foreground session captures nearly all long-lived benefit.
- Per-instance locking does not improve representative concurrency.
- A worker adds IPC without a security or blocking consumer.
- An async runtime adds dependency and debugging cost without simpler correctness.
- Mid-transition preemption is not needed by a representative workload.
- A persistent cache does not beat in-memory session reuse.
- Core validation or lowering is not a dominant term.
- Interpreter execution is not a dominant term.
- Bytecode does not improve complete workload enough to pay verifier and format cost.
- A baseline compiler does not recover compile cost under realistic repetition.
- An optimizing tier has no stable hot workload.
- A new language feature is only scaffolding for runtime metadata.
- A database is justified only by synthetic history.
- Compaction does not cross explicit retained-byte or replay thresholds.
- Grant mutation has no long-lived consumer.
- Multi-user authorization has no deployment consumer.
- OS cgroup integration requires privileged setup without a current operator.
- Provider trials expose no stable telemetry.
- A generator creates another mutable fact owner.
- A source split increases cross-file reads or duplicates invariants.
- An abstraction has one consumer and obscures direct ownership.
- A test framework is larger than the risk it controls.
- A benchmark cannot be reproduced.
- A claimed improvement exists only after excluding setup that users actually pay.
- A prototype requires weakening the interpreter oracle.
- A prototype requires keeping compatibility paths.
- A prototype cannot state a deletion or reversal condition.
- A new dependency cannot justify its supply-chain and build cost.

When a stop rule fires:

- record the evidence;
- delete the losing path;
- update the roadmap gate;
- and continue with the smaller coherent architecture.

## 22. Documentation and fact ownership

Update one owner for every changed fact.

Expected owners include:

- `docs/spec/semantic-model.md` for accepted semantic identity changes;
- `docs/spec/language.md` for observable type and execution changes;
- `docs/spec/reusable-release.md` if reusable interface declarations change release meaning;
- `docs/spec/application.md` for world imports, exports, tests, and artifact;
- `docs/spec/instance.md` for generic commands, outcomes, grants, replay, and history;
- `docs/spec/protocol.md` for runtime session and command contracts;
- a new narrowly named specification only when no existing owner fits;
- `docs/architecture.md` for runtime kernel, topology, dependencies, and TCB;
- `docs/status.md` for implemented presence and exact absence;
- `docs/performance.md` for measurements and selected alternatives;
- `docs/roadmap.md` for unimplemented evidence gates;
- `README.md` for concise public explanation;
- and structured evidence for reproducible machine facts.

Do not place normative behavior only in this prompt.

Do not duplicate active version tables.

Do not claim a JIT, scheduler, supervisor, sandbox, multi-user deployment, or production readiness
unless implemented and verified.

Use terminology consistently.

Explain `runtime kernel`, `runtime supervisor`, `execution engine`, `host interface`, `host adapter`,
and `grant` in the README only if they are retained public concepts.

Keep the README concise.

Keep detailed failure contracts in specifications.

Keep measurements out of normative specifications.

Keep historical narrative out of status.

Keep future aspirations out of implemented status.

## 23. Completion bar

The campaign is complete only when every applicable gate below passes.

### 23.1 Application-world closure

- The application stateful ABI is no longer activation-specific.
- Complete and suspended results are typed.
- Commands and outcomes are exact typed values.
- Imported host interfaces are explicit.
- Application bytes contain requirements but no grants.
- Interface routing is deterministic.
- Foreign interface values reject.
- Old application formats reject directly.
- Application tests cover event and resume behavior.
- Pure invocation remains host-free.

### 23.2 Instance closure

- Generic typed commands persist.
- Generic typed outcomes persist.
- Replay reproduces exact routing and values.
- Event and resume idempotency remain exact.
- Stale bases reject.
- One pending command remains explicit.
- Unknown visibility remains explicit.
- Cross-instance and cross-interface substitution reject.
- Old instance formats reject directly.
- Inspection remains bounded.

### 23.3 Adapter closure

- Activation is one adapter, not generic runtime semantics.
- A second production adapter is retained.
- Each has a disjoint deterministic fake.
- Each has a narrow grant.
- Attempt and outcome behavior is exact.
- Reconciliation or idempotency is exact.
- Corruption and foreign evidence reject.
- Production cannot inject fake outcomes.
- Fake cannot call production operations.

### 23.4 Application closure

- Durable controller works through public boundaries.
- Durable blob publisher works through public boundaries.
- Source workspaces can be removed.
- Standalone releases can be removed when embedded.
- Restart works.
- Duplicate delivery works.
- Stale base rejects.
- Denied authority rejects.
- Unknown and reconciliation work where applicable.
- Tombstone or deletion rules work.
- Identity reuse rejects.

### 23.5 Runtime-kernel closure

- One-shot operations use the common kernel.
- Resource admission is explicit.
- Stage telemetry is available and bounded.
- Cache behavior is derived and bounded.
- Runtime state is reconstructible.
- No hidden current authority exists.
- Shutdown behavior is tested.
- Overload behavior is tested.

### 23.6 Topology closure

- One-shot baseline is measured.
- Foreground session is measured.
- Resident supervisor is either retained with full contract or deleted.
- No unsupported daemon or socket code remains.
- No topology duplicates semantic implementation.
- Process boundaries are not called sandboxes.

### 23.7 Execution closure

- The interpreter or simple route remains an oracle.
- Cold and warm stages are separated.
- Any retained cache has exact invalidation.
- Any retained bytecode or native tier passes differential tests.
- Losing execution prototypes are deleted.
- Performance claims use complete workflows.
- Resource semantics remain equivalent.

### 23.8 Agent-economy closure

- Frozen tasks have deterministic validators.
- At least one development cost improves.
- No token claim is inferred from bytes.
- Agent output remains bounded.
- Interface authoring does not require magic tags.
- Diagnosis names exact stage and authority.
- Losing surfaces are deleted.

### 23.9 Repository closure

- Specifications match behavior.
- Status matches implementation.
- Architecture matches topology.
- Performance evidence is reproducible.
- Roadmap contains only unresolved gates.
- README is accurate.
- Old formats, commands, tests, docs, and prompts without consumers are deleted.
- Full checks pass.
- Public workflows pass.
- `git diff --check` passes.
- Claims are no stronger than evidence.

## 24. Required validation

Run focused checks during development.

Before final handoff run:

```sh
cargo fmt --all -- --check
cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --all-features --locked -- -D warnings
cargo test --workspace --all-targets --all-features --locked
cargo build --workspace --release --locked
git diff --check
```

Run every retained public example.

Run every new runtime topology workflow.

Run exact old-format rejection tests.

Run deterministic decoder mutation tests.

Run exact and one-over policy tests.

Run process-crash and response-loss tests.

Run lock and overload tests.

Run cache corruption and eviction tests.

Run cross-tier differential tests for every retained tier.

Run Miri on the highest-risk safe-Rust ownership, instance, adapter, and runtime paths when available.

Use sanitizers or fuzzing when available and relevant.

Do not claim they ran when unavailable.

Record:

- command;
- exit status;
- elapsed time;
- discovered tests;
- passed tests;
- ignored tests;
- unavailable tools;
- and any environmental exception.

A skipped public workflow is incomplete.

## 25. Final handoff

The final handoff must be compact enough for the next agent to use without rereading this prompt.

Report:

- exact starting commit;
- exact ending worktree or commit;
- unrelated work preserved;
- selected application-world model;
- selected interface identity model;
- selected grant model;
- selected runtime-kernel boundary;
- retained topology;
- rejected topology alternatives;
- selected resource categories;
- retained caches;
- retained execution tiers;
- deleted formats and commands;
- migrated applications;
- new application;
- production adapters;
- fake adapters;
- changed specifications;
- changed source owners;
- validation commands and results;
- public workflow results;
- cold and warm measurements;
- process and engine-open counts;
- resource observations;
- agent-task results;
- actual provider telemetry when exposed;
- exact unsupported claims;
- remaining gates;
- reversal conditions;
- and every requested or implied action not performed.

Do not end with vague statements such as `all tests pass` without command and scope.

Do not describe a prototype as production.

Do not describe a process boundary as isolation.

Do not describe bytes as tokens.

Do not describe a digest as authority.

Do not describe a cache as semantic state.

Do not describe a scheduler as deterministic semantics.

Do not describe this prompt as normative after the campaign ends.

## Appendix A. Decision record template

- `A01` `Question`: State one decision in falsifiable terms.
- `A02` `Consumer`: Name the current application, operator, agent task, or workload that needs it.
- `A03` `Authority`: Name every semantic, durable, operational, and deployment authority affected.
- `A04` `Baseline`: Record exact checkout, command, inputs, environment, and reproduced behavior.
- `A05` `Invariant`: List behavior that may not change.
- `A06` `Threat`: List new attacker-controlled inputs and trust assumptions.
- `A07` `Resource`: List logical, retained, peak, and operational resources.
- `A08` `Alternative`: Include deletion and the smallest local solution.
- `A09` `Prototype`: Build only enough to decide the uncertainty.
- `A10` `Oracle`: Name an independent route that decides correctness.
- `A11` `Metric`: Use complete-workflow and stage measurements.
- `A12` `Threshold`: Write the retention threshold before final measurement.
- `A13` `Failure`: Enumerate publication, crash, unknown, and overload behavior.
- `A14` `Agent`: Measure context, correction, and diagnosis consequences.
- `A15` `Build`: Measure dependency, clean build, incremental build, and binary effects.
- `A16` `Cutover`: List old paths deleted when this option wins.
- `A17` `Stop`: State the condition that stops further work.
- `A18` `Reversal`: State evidence that would reopen the decision.
- `A19` `Result`: Record selected and rejected choices without overstating certainty.
- `A20` `Owner`: Update one specification, one implementation owner, and one evidence owner.

Use one record per substantial architecture choice.
Do not combine several independent decisions into one favorable narrative.

## Appendix B. Public-boundary validation matrix

### B01. workspace JSON request envelope

- `B01.01` `canonical success`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, accept one canonical minimum valid value and reproduce byte-identical meaning.
- `B01.02` `repeated success`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, repeat the exact value and prove the documented replay or equality behavior.
- `B01.03` `unknown field`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject one unknown field without best-effort acceptance.
- `B01.04` `duplicate field`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject every duplicate field even when values are equal.
- `B01.05` `unknown variant`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject an unrecognized tag before semantic work.
- `B01.06` `wrong version`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject every unsupported version directly.
- `B01.07` `wrong domain`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject a valid identity or digest from another domain.
- `B01.08` `noncanonical spelling`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject alternate spellings that would create multiple accepted encodings.
- `B01.09` `truncation`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject every relevant truncation point without panic or partial publication.
- `B01.10` `trailing data`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject trailing bytes, JSON, records, or frames.
- `B01.11` `exact limit`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, accept the exact documented maximum when otherwise valid.
- `B01.12` `one-over limit`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject one unit over the maximum before corresponding allocation or work.
- `B01.13` `empty value`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, accept or reject empty input according to one explicit rule.
- `B01.14` `foreign instance`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject substitution from another instance when the boundary is instance-bound.
- `B01.15` `foreign application`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject substitution from another exact application when application-bound.
- `B01.16` `foreign interface`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject request or outcome substitution from another interface.
- `B01.17` `foreign grant`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject authority substitution before host action.
- `B01.18` `corrupt digest`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, reject content with a recomputed or mismatched digest according to the domain contract.
- `B01.19` `response loss`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, prove publication and idempotent receipt behavior after output failure.
- `B01.20` `resource failure`: For the workspace JSON request envelope, return the exact policy or operational class without hidden partial success.
- `B01.21` `owner`: Name the one executable owner of the workspace JSON request envelope.
- `B01.22` `oracle`: Name the independent test oracle for the workspace JSON request envelope.
- `B01.23` `publication`: State whether the workspace JSON request envelope can publish durable authority.
- `B01.24` `bounds`: Inspect and test every count, depth, byte, work, and output bound for the workspace JSON request envelope.

### B02. editable semantic document

- `B02.01` `canonical success`: For the editable semantic document, accept one canonical minimum valid value and reproduce byte-identical meaning.
- `B02.02` `repeated success`: For the editable semantic document, repeat the exact value and prove the documented replay or equality behavior.
- `B02.03` `unknown field`: For the editable semantic document, reject one unknown field without best-effort acceptance.
- `B02.04` `duplicate field`: For the editable semantic document, reject every duplicate field even when values are equal.
- `B02.05` `unknown variant`: For the editable semantic document, reject an unrecognized tag before semantic work.
- `B02.06` `wrong version`: For the editable semantic document, reject every unsupported version directly.
- `B02.07` `wrong domain`: For the editable semantic document, reject a valid identity or digest from another domain.
- `B02.08` `noncanonical spelling`: For the editable semantic document, reject alternate spellings that would create multiple accepted encodings.
- `B02.09` `truncation`: For the editable semantic document, reject every relevant truncation point without panic or partial publication.
- `B02.10` `trailing data`: For the editable semantic document, reject trailing bytes, JSON, records, or frames.
- `B02.11` `exact limit`: For the editable semantic document, accept the exact documented maximum when otherwise valid.
- `B02.12` `one-over limit`: For the editable semantic document, reject one unit over the maximum before corresponding allocation or work.

## Appendix J. End-to-end scenario catalogue

### J01. build and run a pure application after source deletion

- `J01.01` Name exact starting authority and all input digests.
- `J01.02` Run through supported public commands rather than private Rust fixtures.
- `J01.03` Record every process, runtime open, and adapter call.
- `J01.04` Record exact expected state revision and pending-command status.
- `J01.05` Record expected publication and response-loss behavior.
- `J01.06` Restart at the most relevant boundary.
- `J01.07` Inspect bounded exact state after restart.
- `J01.08` Run deterministic replay without host work.
- `J01.09` Verify cross-domain substitution rejects.
- `J01.10` Record resource reservations and releases.
- `J01.11` Record stage telemetry.
- `J01.12` State the independent oracle.

## Appendix L. External primary-source orientation

External systems are orientation, not architecture mandates.

Verify current primary sources when internet access is available.

Consult only to sharpen comparisons.

### L.1 Erlang/OTP runtime scheduling

Primary orientation:

- <https://www.erlang.org/doc/apps/erts/erlang.html>
- <https://www.erlang.org/doc/apps/erts/erl_cmd.html>

Study:

- reduction-based preemption;
- per-priority queues;
- scheduler counts;
- dirty schedulers;
- CPU quota awareness;
- and separation between process semantics and runtime scheduling.

Do not copy the BEAM process model without a current lkjscript actor or mailbox consumer.

### L.2 Wasmtime embedding and resource control

Primary orientation:

- <https://docs.wasmtime.dev/api/wasmtime/>
- <https://docs.wasmtime.dev/api/wasmtime/struct.Config.html>
- <https://docs.wasmtime.dev/api/wasmtime/struct.PoolingAllocationConfig.html>

Study:

- shareable engine and compiled modules;
- cold compilation versus reusable compiled units;
- async embedding responsibility;
- deterministic fuel versus epoch interruption;
- pooling allocation;
- and limits that do not cover all host memory.

Do not embed Wasmtime without an interoperability consumer.

### L.3 V8 tiering and embedding

Primary orientation:

- <https://v8.dev/blog/sparkplug>
- <https://v8.dev/blog/maglev>
- <https://v8.dev/docs/embed>

Study:

- baseline compilation;
- intermediate tier tradeoffs;
- compilation speed versus peak speed;
- contexts and isolation domains;
- snapshots;
- and startup caching.

Do not copy V8's tier count, mutable language assumptions, or GC architecture.

### L.4 WebAssembly Component Model and WASI

Primary orientation:

- <https://component-model.bytecodealliance.org/design/worlds.html>
- <https://component-model.bytecodealliance.org/design/wit.html>
- <https://component-model.bytecodealliance.org/design/async.html>
- <https://wasi.dev/>

Study:

- explicit imports and exports;
- worlds;
- capability-oriented host fulfillment;
- typed resources;
- futures and streams;
- and runtime scheduling responsibility.

Use these ideas to test terminology and interface closure.

Do not add live resources, async semantics, or a component runtime without a current consumer.

### L.5 systemd and OS deployment control

Primary orientation:

- <https://systemd.io/>
- <https://systemd.io/TRANSIENT-SETTINGS/>

Study:

- explicit service lifecycle;
- socket activation;
- cgroup-backed CPU, memory, task, and I/O controls;
- transient units;
- and separation between application logic and deployment policy.

Do not make systemd a semantic dependency.

### L.6 Research discipline

For every external comparison:

- record the exact source and access date;
- identify the problem dimension, not merely the product name;
- state which assumptions differ from lkjscript;
- state what was learned;
- state what was rejected;
- and do not claim inherited correctness.

End of active campaign prompt.
Execute from the actual checkout, update the compact ledger, and leave one coherent verified architecture.
